I was trying to be helpful, but it’s gotten to be a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the fundraiser project excessively, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it was hard to shut it off. My mind would jump from quilt blocks to donor lists to the church announcement, and then back again so fast that I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I kept telling myself it was just being organized, but… it was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or cleaning, that feels like it's getting in the way of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Not cleaning, exactly… I wouldn’t say it was like that. But my husband did point out that I was pacing around the sewing room a lot, sorting the same fabric piles again and again, like I was being productive. And then other mornings I’d be the opposite—just so slow that even making coffee felt like a big chore. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe both? I was trying to think about it on purpose, to be useful and keep things moving. But then it would sort of take off on its own. My mind would jump from the quilt blocks to the donor list to the church announcement so quickly that I couldn’t really stay with one thought long enough to finish it. I don’t know if that counts as intrusive, exactly… but it didn’t feel very easy to control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with how you typically think about things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do like to be organized and think things through, but this felt faster than my usual way of thinking. My mind would race from the quilt blocks to the donor lists to the church announcements, and I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about the fundraiser project that you're trying to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was trying to push them away, exactly. It wasn’t like I’d think, “Stop thinking about this,” and then couldn’t. It was more that they just kept coming up because I felt like I should be helping with it. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors in a specific way to feel right or to reduce anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’m with the historical society, and I took on this fundraiser project like I could just manage the whole thing myself. My mind would jump from quilt blocks to donor lists to the church announcement, and then back again so fast that I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I kept telling myself it was just being organized, but… it was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or cleaning, that feels like it's getting in the way of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Not cleaning, exactly… I wouldn’t say it was like that. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe both? I don’t know if that counts as intrusive, exactly… but it didn’t feel very easy to control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with how you typically think about things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do like to be organized and think things through, but this felt faster than my usual way of thinking. And he’s even had to deal with people from the historical society, because I couldn’t face them when I knew they were waiting on me.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be irresponsible. I just… stopped following through the way I normally would, and then I felt too ashamed to talk to anyone about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop or significantly cut back on any activities, including work or social events, because of your thoughts or feelings about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have cut back, more than I wanted to. I’m not working, so it’s not like I missed a job or anything, but at home my husband has had to take over routines I normally keep up with. And with the historical society, I just stopped keeping up with what I’d promised, and then I couldn’t bring myself to face the people who were waiting on me.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I just didn’t care. I did care. I just… couldn’t seem to follow through. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are consistent with your own values and beliefs?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep going over every late order and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, telling myself I let the church, the historical society, and this whole town down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I think in a way they do, because I do value keeping my word and being kind to people. So when I keep going over every late order, or every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, it feels like proof that I didn’t live up to that.

But maybe I take it too far. I keep telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and really the whole town… and I know that probably sounds extreme. I don’t want to be unfair to myself, but it feels consistent with what I believe I should have done, if that makes sense.

My mind would jump from quilt blocks to donor lists to the church announcement, and then back again so fast that I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I got myself a little overcommitted. I’m with the historical society, and I took on this fundraiser project like I could just manage the whole thing myself. I started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, setting up schedules… and I may have promised some deadlines that, looking back, weren’t very realistic. I was trying to be helpful, but it’s gotten to be a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the fundraiser project excessively, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it was hard to shut it off. My mind would jump from quilt blocks to donor lists to the church announcement, and then back again so fast that I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I kept telling myself it was just being organized, but… it was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or cleaning, that feels like it's getting in the way of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Not cleaning, exactly… I wouldn’t say it was like that. But my husband did point out that I was pacing around the sewing room a lot, sorting the same fabric piles again and again, like I was being productive. And then other mornings I’d be the opposite—just so slow that even making coffee felt like a big chore. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe both? I was trying to think about it on purpose, to be useful and keep things moving. But then it would sort of take off on its own. My mind would jump from the quilt blocks to the donor list to the church announcement so quickly that I couldn’t really stay with one thought long enough to finish it. I don’t know if that counts as intrusive, exactly… but it didn’t feel very easy to control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with how you typically think about things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do like to be organized and think things through, but this felt faster than my usual way of thinking. My mind would race from the quilt blocks to the donor lists to the church announcements, and I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about the fundraiser project that you're trying to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was trying to push them away, exactly. It wasn’t like I’d think, “Stop thinking about this,” and then couldn’t. It was more that they just kept coming up because I felt like I should be helping with it. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors in a specific way to feel right or to reduce anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I like things done neatly, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something a certain number of times or do it just right to calm myself down. At least, I haven’t really noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about the fundraiser project is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: A lot of distress… yes, I guess it is, though I hate to make it sound dramatic. I keep going back over the late orders and the things I said too sharply to the volunteers, and then I start telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society too… really the whole town. It feels awful to think that. I know that may sound like I’m being too hard on myself, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about the fundraiser project and its related distress been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit. My husband has been very kind about it, but he’s had to take over a lot of the usual household things because I just wasn’t keeping up with them. And he’s even had to deal with people from the historical society, because I couldn’t face them when I knew they were waiting on me.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be irresponsible. I just… stopped following through the way I normally would, and then I felt too ashamed to talk to anyone about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop or significantly cut back on any activities, including work or social events, because of your thoughts or feelings about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have cut back, more than I wanted to. I’m not working, so it’s not like I missed a job or anything, but at home my husband has had to take over routines I normally keep up with. And with the historical society, I just stopped keeping up with what I’d promised, and then I couldn’t bring myself to face the people who were waiting on me.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I just didn’t care. I did care. I just… couldn’t seem to follow through. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are consistent with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: I… I think in a way they do, because I do value keeping my word and being kind to people. So when I keep going over every late order, or every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, it feels like proof that I didn’t live up to that.\n\nBut maybe I take it too far. I keep telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and really the whole town… and I know that probably sounds extreme. I don’t want to be unfair to myself, but it feels consistent with what I believe I should have done, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific rituals or behaviors that you must perform in order to reduce your anxiety or distress about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head more than I probably should, but I don’t have a specific thing I have to do, like checking something a certain number of times or anything like that.\n\nMostly I just feel awful and try to avoid stirring it up. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… my sleep has been off. I was going to say “a little,” but that’s probably not quite honest.

For a while there, I was only sleeping a few hours and then waking up like I needed to get right to sewing or making phone calls about the fundraiser. And now it’s different—I’ll lie there worrying over it, or I’ll sleep late and still wake up feeling worn out.

I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but yes, it has been affecting my sleep.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I got myself a little overcommitted. I’m with the historical society, and I took on this fundraiser project like I could just manage the whole thing myself. I started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, setting up schedules… and I may have promised some deadlines that, looking back, weren’t very realistic. I was trying to be helpful, but it’s gotten to be a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the fundraiser project excessively, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it was hard to shut it off. My mind would jump from quilt blocks to donor lists to the church announcement, and then back again so fast that I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I kept telling myself it was just being organized, but… it was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or cleaning, that feels like it's getting in the way of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Not cleaning, exactly… I wouldn’t say it was like that. But my husband did point out that I was pacing around the sewing room a lot, sorting the same fabric piles again and again, like I was being productive. And then other mornings I’d be the opposite—just so slow that even making coffee felt like a big chore. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe both? I was trying to think about it on purpose, to be useful and keep things moving. But then it would sort of take off on its own. My mind would jump from the quilt blocks to the donor list to the church announcement so quickly that I couldn’t really stay with one thought long enough to finish it. I don’t know if that counts as intrusive, exactly… but it didn’t feel very easy to control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with how you typically think about things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do like to be organized and think things through, but this felt faster than my usual way of thinking. My mind would race from the quilt blocks to the donor lists to the church announcements, and I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about the fundraiser project that you're trying to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was trying to push them away, exactly. It wasn’t like I’d think, “Stop thinking about this,” and then couldn’t. It was more that they just kept coming up because I felt like I should be helping with it. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors in a specific way to feel right or to reduce anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I like things done neatly, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something a certain number of times or do it just right to calm myself down. At least, I haven’t really noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about the fundraiser project is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: A lot of distress… yes, I guess it is, though I hate to make it sound dramatic. I keep going back over the late orders and the things I said too sharply to the volunteers, and then I start telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society too… really the whole town. It feels awful to think that. I know that may sound like I’m being too hard on myself, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about the fundraiser project and its related distress been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit. My husband has been very kind about it, but he’s had to take over a lot of the usual household things because I just wasn’t keeping up with them. And he’s even had to deal with people from the historical society, because I couldn’t face them when I knew they were waiting on me.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be irresponsible. I just… stopped following through the way I normally would, and then I felt too ashamed to talk to anyone about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop or significantly cut back on any activities, including work or social events, because of your thoughts or feelings about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have cut back, more than I wanted to. I’m not working, so it’s not like I missed a job or anything, but at home my husband has had to take over routines I normally keep up with. And with the historical society, I just stopped keeping up with what I’d promised, and then I couldn’t bring myself to face the people who were waiting on me.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I just didn’t care. I did care. I just… couldn’t seem to follow through. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are consistent with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: I… I think in a way they do, because I do value keeping my word and being kind to people. So when I keep going over every late order, or every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, it feels like proof that I didn’t live up to that.\n\nBut maybe I take it too far. I keep telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and really the whole town… and I know that probably sounds extreme. I don’t want to be unfair to myself, but it feels consistent with what I believe I should have done, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific rituals or behaviors that you must perform in order to reduce your anxiety or distress about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head more than I probably should, but I don’t have a specific thing I have to do, like checking something a certain number of times or anything like that.\n\nMostly I just feel awful and try to avoid stirring it up. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular behavior I must do to make the anxiety go down. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of your thoughts about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep has been off. I was going to say “a little,” but that’s probably not quite honest.\n\nFor a while there, I was only sleeping a few hours and then waking up like I needed to get right to sewing or making phone calls about the fundraiser. And now it’s different—I’ll lie there worrying over it, or I’ll sleep late and still wake up feeling worn out.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but yes, it has been affecting my sleep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… mostly they fit with how I usually think, at least the part about wanting to do right by people. I’ve always cared about being dependable.

But the way I keep circling it now feels heavier than usual. I keep going over every late order and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, and then I end up telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society… honestly, the whole town. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular behavior I must do to make the anxiety go down. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of your thoughts about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep has been off. I was going to say “a little,” but that’s probably not quite honest.\n\nFor a while there, I was only sleeping a few hours and then waking up like I needed to get right to sewing or making phone calls about the fundraiser. And now it’s different—I’ll lie there worrying over it, or I’ll sleep late and still wake up feeling worn out.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but yes, it has been affecting my sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are something that you would normally think, or are they foreign to your typical way of thinking?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly they fit with how I usually think, at least the part about wanting to do right by people. I’ve always cared about being dependable.\n\nBut the way I keep circling it now feels heavier than usual. I keep going over every late order and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, and then I end up telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society… honestly, the whole town. Saying it out loud, I know that may be more than what’s reasonable.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it feels foreign exactly, just… like my normal sense of responsibility turned up too high. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have to think about the fundraiser project in a specific way to feel right or to reduce anxiety?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I have to think about it in a specific way, no. I mean, I do replay parts of it and try to make sense of what I should’ve done better, but it’s not like there’s a certain phrase or order I have to go through to feel right.

I guess I’m mostly just worrying, if that makes sense. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re looking for, but I haven’t noticed a set way I have to think about it to calm down.

I don’t know if that counts as intrusive, exactly… but it didn’t feel very easy to control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with how you typically think about things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do like to be organized and think things through, but this felt faster than my usual way of thinking. I kept telling myself it was just being organized, but… it was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or cleaning, that feels like it's getting in the way of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Not cleaning, exactly… I wouldn’t say it was like that. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about the fundraiser project that you're trying to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was trying to push them away, exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say they feel like me… not like something put into my head or anything like that. I don’t feel taken over by them.

It’s more that it’s my own guilt getting very loud. I keep going back over the late orders, and the times I was sharp with the volunteers, and then I tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and, well… the whole town. I know that may sound like I’m making too much of it.

But no, it doesn’t feel alien. It feels like my own thoughts, just harsher than I’d like.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I got myself a little overcommitted. I’m with the historical society, and I took on this fundraiser project like I could just manage the whole thing myself. I started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, setting up schedules… and I may have promised some deadlines that, looking back, weren’t very realistic. I was trying to be helpful, but it’s gotten to be a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the fundraiser project excessively, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it was hard to shut it off. My mind would jump from quilt blocks to donor lists to the church announcement, and then back again so fast that I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I kept telling myself it was just being organized, but… it was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or cleaning, that feels like it's getting in the way of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Not cleaning, exactly… I wouldn’t say it was like that. But my husband did point out that I was pacing around the sewing room a lot, sorting the same fabric piles again and again, like I was being productive. And then other mornings I’d be the opposite—just so slow that even making coffee felt like a big chore. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe both? I was trying to think about it on purpose, to be useful and keep things moving. But then it would sort of take off on its own. My mind would jump from the quilt blocks to the donor list to the church announcement so quickly that I couldn’t really stay with one thought long enough to finish it. I don’t know if that counts as intrusive, exactly… but it didn’t feel very easy to control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with how you typically think about things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do like to be organized and think things through, but this felt faster than my usual way of thinking. My mind would race from the quilt blocks to the donor lists to the church announcements, and I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about the fundraiser project that you're trying to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was trying to push them away, exactly. It wasn’t like I’d think, “Stop thinking about this,” and then couldn’t. It was more that they just kept coming up because I felt like I should be helping with it. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors in a specific way to feel right or to reduce anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I like things done neatly, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something a certain number of times or do it just right to calm myself down. At least, I haven’t really noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about the fundraiser project is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: A lot of distress… yes, I guess it is, though I hate to make it sound dramatic. I keep going back over the late orders and the things I said too sharply to the volunteers, and then I start telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society too… really the whole town. It feels awful to think that. I know that may sound like I’m being too hard on myself, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about the fundraiser project and its related distress been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit. My husband has been very kind about it, but he’s had to take over a lot of the usual household things because I just wasn’t keeping up with them. And he’s even had to deal with people from the historical society, because I couldn’t face them when I knew they were waiting on me.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be irresponsible. I just… stopped following through the way I normally would, and then I felt too ashamed to talk to anyone about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop or significantly cut back on any activities, including work or social events, because of your thoughts or feelings about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have cut back, more than I wanted to. I’m not working, so it’s not like I missed a job or anything, but at home my husband has had to take over routines I normally keep up with. And with the historical society, I just stopped keeping up with what I’d promised, and then I couldn’t bring myself to face the people who were waiting on me.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I just didn’t care. I did care. I just… couldn’t seem to follow through. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are consistent with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: I… I think in a way they do, because I do value keeping my word and being kind to people. So when I keep going over every late order, or every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, it feels like proof that I didn’t live up to that.\n\nBut maybe I take it too far. I keep telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and really the whole town… and I know that probably sounds extreme. I don’t want to be unfair to myself, but it feels consistent with what I believe I should have done, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific rituals or behaviors that you must perform in order to reduce your anxiety or distress about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head more than I probably should, but I don’t have a specific thing I have to do, like checking something a certain number of times or anything like that.\n\nMostly I just feel awful and try to avoid stirring it up. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular behavior I must do to make the anxiety go down. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of your thoughts about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep has been off. I was going to say “a little,” but that’s probably not quite honest.\n\nFor a while there, I was only sleeping a few hours and then waking up like I needed to get right to sewing or making phone calls about the fundraiser. And now it’s different—I’ll lie there worrying over it, or I’ll sleep late and still wake up feeling worn out.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but yes, it has been affecting my sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are something that you would normally think, or are they foreign to your typical way of thinking?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly they fit with how I usually think, at least the part about wanting to do right by people. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe both? I was trying to think about it on purpose, to be useful and keep things moving. But then it would sort of take off on its own. My mind would jump from the quilt blocks to the donor list to the church announcement so quickly that I couldn’t really stay with one thought long enough to finish it. I don’t know if that counts as intrusive, exactly… but it didn’t feel very easy to control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with how you typically think about things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do like to be organized and think things through, but this felt faster than my usual way of thinking. My mind would race from the quilt blocks to the donor lists to the church announcements, and I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about the fundraiser project that you're trying to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was trying to push them away, exactly. It wasn’t like I’d think, “Stop thinking about this,” and then couldn’t. It was more that they just kept coming up because I felt like I should be helping with it. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors in a specific way to feel right or to reduce anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I like things done neatly, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something a certain number of times or do it just right to calm myself down. At least, I haven’t really noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about the fundraiser project is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: A lot of distress… yes, I guess it is, though I hate to make it sound dramatic. I keep going back over the late orders and the things I said too sharply to the volunteers, and then I start telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society too… really the whole town. It feels awful to think that. I know that may sound like I’m being too hard on myself, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about the fundraiser project and its related distress been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit. My husband has been very kind about it, but he’s had to take over a lot of the usual household things because I just wasn’t keeping up with them. And he’s even had to deal with people from the historical society, because I couldn’t face them when I knew they were waiting on me.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be irresponsible. I just… stopped following through the way I normally would, and then I felt too ashamed to talk to anyone about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop or significantly cut back on any activities, including work or social events, because of your thoughts or feelings about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have cut back, more than I wanted to. I’m not working, so it’s not like I missed a job or anything, but at home my husband has had to take over routines I normally keep up with. And with the historical society, I just stopped keeping up with what I’d promised, and then I couldn’t bring myself to face the people who were waiting on me.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I just didn’t care. I did care. I just… couldn’t seem to follow through. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are consistent with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: I… I think in a way they do, because I do value keeping my word and being kind to people. So when I keep going over every late order, or every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, it feels like proof that I didn’t live up to that.\n\nBut maybe I take it too far. I keep telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and really the whole town… and I know that probably sounds extreme. I don’t want to be unfair to myself, but it feels consistent with what I believe I should have done, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific rituals or behaviors that you must perform in order to reduce your anxiety or distress about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head more than I probably should, but I don’t have a specific thing I have to do, like checking something a certain number of times or anything like that.\n\nMostly I just feel awful and try to avoid stirring it up. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular behavior I must do to make the anxiety go down. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of your thoughts about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep has been off. I was going to say “a little,” but that’s probably not quite honest.\n\nFor a while there, I was only sleeping a few hours and then waking up like I needed to get right to sewing or making phone calls about the fundraiser. And now it’s different—I’ll lie there worrying over it, or I’ll sleep late and still wake up feeling worn out.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but yes, it has been affecting my sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are something that you would normally think, or are they foreign to your typical way of thinking?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly they fit with how I usually think, at least the part about wanting to do right by people. I’ve always cared about being dependable.\n\nBut the way I keep circling it now feels heavier than usual. I keep going over every late order and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, and then I end up telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society… honestly, the whole town. Saying it out loud, I know that may be more than what’s reasonable.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it feels foreign exactly, just… like my normal sense of responsibility turned up too high. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about the fundraiser project in a specific way to feel right or to reduce anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have to think about it in a specific way, no. I mean, I do replay parts of it and try to make sense of what I should’ve done better, but it’s not like there’s a certain phrase or order I have to go through to feel right.\n\nI guess I’m mostly just worrying, if that makes sense. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re looking for, but I haven’t noticed a set way I have to think about it to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I don’t like how intense it’s gotten, but caring about the fundraiser and not wanting to disappoint people, that does feel like me.\n\nIt’s just that I keep going over every late order, and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, and then I start telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and… really the whole town. Which sounds dramatic when I say it, I know. I don’t want to make too much of it. But yes, it’s in line with who I am, just taken to a pretty hard place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are something that you would consider 'you' or are they something that feel alien or imposed on you?\n\nPatient: I’d say they feel like me… not like something put into my head or anything like that. I don’t feel taken over by them.\n\nIt’s more that it’s my own guilt getting very loud. I keep going back over the late orders, and the times I was sharp with the volunteers, and then I tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and, well… the whole town. I know that may sound like I’m making too much of it.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t feel alien. It feels like my own thoughts, just harsher than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are something you'd consider a part of yourself or something that doesn't fit with how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: I think they’re part of me, yes… maybe not the fairest part, but they do fit with how I see myself as someone who should’ve done better.\n\nI keep going over every late order and every time I was sharp with the volunteers, and then I tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and honestly the whole town. And now it’s different—I’ll lie there worrying over it, or I’ll sleep late and still wake up feeling worn out.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but yes, it has been affecting my sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are something that you would normally think, or are they foreign to your typical way of thinking?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly they fit with how I usually think, at least the part about wanting to do right by people. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose… it’s a bit of both, if that’s okay to say.

I do think of myself as someone who tries to be dependable and kind, so when I keep going over every late order and every time I was sharp with the volunteers, it really doesn’t match who I want to be. I start telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and… this whole town, really.

I know that probably sounds too harsh, and I’m not trying to make myself out to be awful. It’s just that the guilt feels like it fits somehow, even if it’s more than I should be carrying.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I got myself a little overcommitted. I’m with the historical society, and I took on this fundraiser project like I could just manage the whole thing myself. I started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, setting up schedules… and I may have promised some deadlines that, looking back, weren’t very realistic. I was trying to be helpful, but it’s gotten to be a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the fundraiser project excessively, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it was hard to shut it off. My mind would jump from quilt blocks to donor lists to the church announcement, and then back again so fast that I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I kept telling myself it was just being organized, but… it was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or cleaning, that feels like it's getting in the way of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Not cleaning, exactly… I wouldn’t say it was like that. But my husband did point out that I was pacing around the sewing room a lot, sorting the same fabric piles again and again, like I was being productive. And then other mornings I’d be the opposite—just so slow that even making coffee felt like a big chore. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally thinking about?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe both? I was trying to think about it on purpose, to be useful and keep things moving. But then it would sort of take off on its own. My mind would jump from the quilt blocks to the donor list to the church announcement so quickly that I couldn’t really stay with one thought long enough to finish it. I don’t know if that counts as intrusive, exactly… but it didn’t feel very easy to control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with how you typically think about things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I do like to be organized and think things through, but this felt faster than my usual way of thinking. My mind would race from the quilt blocks to the donor lists to the church announcements, and I couldn’t really hold onto one thought long enough to finish it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about the fundraiser project that you're trying to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was trying to push them away, exactly. It wasn’t like I’d think, “Stop thinking about this,” and then couldn’t. It was more that they just kept coming up because I felt like I should be helping with it. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors in a specific way to feel right or to reduce anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I like things done neatly, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something a certain number of times or do it just right to calm myself down. At least, I haven’t really noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about the fundraiser project is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: A lot of distress… yes, I guess it is, though I hate to make it sound dramatic. I keep going back over the late orders and the things I said too sharply to the volunteers, and then I start telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society too… really the whole town. It feels awful to think that. I know that may sound like I’m being too hard on myself, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about the fundraiser project and its related distress been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I’d like to admit. My husband has been very kind about it, but he’s had to take over a lot of the usual household things because I just wasn’t keeping up with them. And he’s even had to deal with people from the historical society, because I couldn’t face them when I knew they were waiting on me.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to be irresponsible. I just… stopped following through the way I normally would, and then I felt too ashamed to talk to anyone about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had to stop or significantly cut back on any activities, including work or social events, because of your thoughts or feelings about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have cut back, more than I wanted to. I’m not working, so it’s not like I missed a job or anything, but at home my husband has had to take over routines I normally keep up with. And with the historical society, I just stopped keeping up with what I’d promised, and then I couldn’t bring myself to face the people who were waiting on me.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I just didn’t care. I did care. I just… couldn’t seem to follow through. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are consistent with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: I… I think in a way they do, because I do value keeping my word and being kind to people. So when I keep going over every late order, or every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, it feels like proof that I didn’t live up to that.\n\nBut maybe I take it too far. I keep telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and really the whole town… and I know that probably sounds extreme. I don’t want to be unfair to myself, but it feels consistent with what I believe I should have done, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific rituals or behaviors that you must perform in order to reduce your anxiety or distress about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not rituals exactly. I mean, I do go over things in my head more than I probably should, but I don’t have a specific thing I have to do, like checking something a certain number of times or anything like that.\n\nMostly I just feel awful and try to avoid stirring it up. But no, I haven’t noticed any particular behavior I must do to make the anxiety go down. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of your thoughts about the fundraiser project?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep has been off. I was going to say “a little,” but that’s probably not quite honest.\n\nFor a while there, I was only sleeping a few hours and then waking up like I needed to get right to sewing or making phone calls about the fundraiser. And now it’s different—I’ll lie there worrying over it, or I’ll sleep late and still wake up feeling worn out.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but yes, it has been affecting my sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are something that you would normally think, or are they foreign to your typical way of thinking?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly they fit with how I usually think, at least the part about wanting to do right by people. I’ve always cared about being dependable.\n\nBut the way I keep circling it now feels heavier than usual. I keep going over every late order and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, and then I end up telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society… honestly, the whole town. Saying it out loud, I know that may be more than what’s reasonable.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it feels foreign exactly, just… like my normal sense of responsibility turned up too high. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about the fundraiser project in a specific way to feel right or to reduce anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have to think about it in a specific way, no. I mean, I do replay parts of it and try to make sense of what I should’ve done better, but it’s not like there’s a certain phrase or order I have to go through to feel right.\n\nI guess I’m mostly just worrying, if that makes sense. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re looking for, but I haven’t noticed a set way I have to think about it to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are in line with who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I don’t like how intense it’s gotten, but caring about the fundraiser and not wanting to disappoint people, that does feel like me.\n\nIt’s just that I keep going over every late order, and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers, and then I start telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and… really the whole town. Which sounds dramatic when I say it, I know. I don’t want to make too much of it. But yes, it’s in line with who I am, just taken to a pretty hard place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are something that you would consider 'you' or are they something that feel alien or imposed on you?\n\nPatient: I’d say they feel like me… not like something put into my head or anything like that. I don’t feel taken over by them.\n\nIt’s more that it’s my own guilt getting very loud. I keep going back over the late orders, and the times I was sharp with the volunteers, and then I tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and, well… the whole town. I know that may sound like I’m making too much of it.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t feel alien. It feels like my own thoughts, just harsher than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about the fundraiser project are something you'd consider a part of yourself or something that doesn't fit with how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: I think they’re part of me, yes… maybe not the fairest part, but they do fit with how I see myself as someone who should’ve done better.\n\nI keep going over every late order and every time I was sharp with the volunteers, and then I tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and honestly the whole town.
